# Review: one procedure for every agent

Status: stable reference (R-0544)

Every branch is reviewed by `scripts/review/review.sh` before it lands. The
script picks the diff, splits it, runs the reviewer, records the verdict as a
git note on the reviewed commit, and enforces the round cap.
`scripts/review/verify.sh` validates that note. The landing tools in `scripts/land/`
([LANDING.md](LANDING.md)) read its verdict through `scripts/land/notes.sh`: a branch is
ready to land only when its head carries a clean review note.

## Why one script

Measured on 2026-10-22:

- Reviews took 8 to 34 rounds per branch, and every round re-reviewed the whole
  growing diff. Most of each round's cost was re-reading code that an earlier
  round had already accepted.
- The reviewer's content filter refused whole rounds on ownership and memory
  diffs. The same diffs went through once the prompt described the work neutrally
  ("a compiler ownership-checker review") and the diff was sent in smaller pieces.

Each agent had been solving both problems by hand, differently. The script does
it the same way every time, and its note is the record the landing train checks.

## Usage

```sh
scripts/review/review.sh [--base <ref>] [--head <ref>] [--max-rounds 5] [--pieces auto|N] [--prompt-extra "..."]
scripts/review/verify.sh [--fetch] <commit>
```

One invocation runs **one round** on `HEAD`. Commit fixes between rounds; the
uncommitted tree is never reviewed, and the script warns when it is dirty.

| option | meaning |
| --- | --- |
| `--base <ref>` | base of round 1. Default: the merge-base of `HEAD` with `origin/main`. |
| `--head <ref>` | the commit the caller means to review; it must be the checkout's HEAD, or nothing runs (exit 2). `train.sh` and `ready.sh` pass it. |
| `--max-rounds N` | the round cap (default 5). |
| `--pieces auto\|N` | `auto` splits at 60000 bytes per piece (`REVIEW_PIECE_BYTES`); `N` aims for N pieces. File groups are never merged, so a diff touching more groups than N still gets one piece per group. |
| `--prompt-extra "..."` | text appended to every prompt, such as what the branch is for. |

| exit | meaning |
| --- | --- |
| 0 | clean |
| 1 | findings: fix, commit, run again |
| 2 | usage or environment error; nothing recorded |
| 3 | round cap reached; split the branch or escalate (open findings are printed) |
| 4 | nothing to review: `HEAD` was already reviewed with findings, or the diff is empty |
| 5 | blocked: a piece could not be reviewed, even after the retry |
| 6 | recorded locally, but the notes ref could not be pushed |

## What each round reviews

- **Round 1** reviews the full diff from the base to `HEAD`.
- **Round N > 1** reviews only the delta since the last reviewed commit. The
  script finds that commit by walking the branch (`base..HEAD`) for the newest
  note under `refs/notes/concrete-review`. The prompt includes the previous
  round's findings and asks the reviewer to list each one that is still open.
- A `blocked` round does not count as having reviewed its diff, so the next
  round starts from the last `clean` or `findings` round, or from the base.
  Running again on a blocked `HEAD` is allowed and counts as a round. A round
  with both findings and a blocked piece is `blocked` for this reason; its
  findings are still recorded and carried into the next prompt, together with
  those of the last fully reviewed round.
- A `HEAD` that is already `clean` exits 0 without calling the reviewer.

Rounds are counted from the notes, so the cap holds across invocations,
worktrees and agents. Rebasing creates new commits without notes, which
restarts the count; say so when you hand the branch over.

## Splitting and the neutral prompt

The diff is split by file group first, in this order: compiler sources
(`Concrete/`, `std/`, `proofs/`, `grammar/`, top-level Lean and Lake files),
tests (`tests/`, `lean_tests/`, `examples/`, `bench/`, `scripts/tests/`),
scripts (everything else), docs (`docs/`, `paper/`, `site/`, `research/`,
`*.md`). Within a group, files are packed into pieces at or under the size
limit. A file larger than the limit is cut at hunk boundaries, and a hunk larger
than the limit is cut into parts with renumbered `@@` headers, so each piece is a
well-formed diff. `scripts/review/split_diff.py` does the splitting.

The prompt describes the repository as a compiler and asks for correctness
findings: wrong behaviour, logic errors, regressions, unhandled cases, tests that
do not check what they claim, and docs that contradict the code. It runs
`codex exec --sandbox read-only` from the repository root, with the piece on
stdin, so the reviewer can open other files for context but cannot change them.
It runs with `GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0`: the sandbox does not stop git from taking the
worktree's `index.lock` for an index refresh, and a reviewer command stopped
mid-refresh left that lock behind (bug 209).

The reply must end with a line that is exactly `VERDICT: clean` or
`VERDICT: findings`. Markdown emphasis around it is tolerated; anything after it
is not. A reply without that line is treated as no answer, never as clean.

**Refusals.** A piece that comes back refused, with no verdict line, or with a
reviewer error is retried **once**: the piece is halved and each half is sent
with a more neutral prompt that leaves out the project framing. If any half
fails again, the piece is `blocked` and the script moves on. It does not loop.
Refusal text is matched only when the reply has no verdict line, so a real
review that discusses content filters still parses.

## Owner decisions: choosing versus recording

Both prompts ask whether the patch picks a side on an owner decision: choosing, revising,
encoding, implementing, extending or reverting what an undecided question covers, whether one of
the language decisions OD1 – OD157 ([decisions.md](decisions.md#owner-decisions)) or an important
project question. That is a finding, and the patch does not land: only the owner decides.

Recording a decision the owner explicitly approved, under the
[recording rule](decisions.md#decided-project-recording), is not picking a side, and the review
permits it. For such a patch the reviewer checks four things, and a failure of any is a finding:

- **provenance**: each recorded answer carries the owner's answer, quoted with its date, or the
  quoted owner rule that decides the question under the recording rule;
- **faithful transcription**: the recorded text says what that answer says, no more and no less;
- **scope**: the patch changes only what that answer covers;
- **nothing unapproved**: nothing else is marked decided, and no undecided question is chosen or
  revised.

A review that refuses such a patch is answered in the next round (a quoted answer, a narrower
change), never by skipping the review.

## The note

After each round the script writes this note on `HEAD` under
`refs/notes/concrete-review`, and pushes the ref to `origin`:

```
verdict: clean|findings|blocked
rounds: N
reviewer: codex
utc: 2026-10-22T14:03:11Z
base: <40-hex sha>
pieces: K
```

**The format is an interface.** `verify.sh` parses it, and the train's review
leg reads it through `verify.sh`, which rejects any note that lacks one of these
six keys, repeats a key, or carries any other key. Keys are matched by name, not
position. Change the format only together with `verify.sh`.

- `rounds` is this round's number, which is also the number of rounds run on the
  branch so far.
- `base` is the branch base (the merge-base), not the start of the delta.
- `pieces` counts the pieces before any refusal retry.

When the verdict is not clean, the findings text goes in a second note under
`refs/notes/concrete-review-findings`, which is pushed too. It is how the next
round, possibly run by another agent, gets the previous findings. The full raw
output of every round is written to `.review/<sha>-<round>.md`, which git
ignores.

A non-fast-forward rejection (another agent pushed notes in the meantime) is
handled by fetching the remote notes, merging them (`git notes merge -s ours`: on
a conflict for the same commit the local, newer note wins), and retrying, up to
three attempts. Any other push failure stops at once with exit 6. The script also
fetches and merges the remote notes before each round, so it counts rounds that
were run elsewhere.

The pre-push hook (`.githooks/pre-push`) runs no gates for a push that updates
only these two notes refs: a notes commit holds one blob per annotated commit and
no source tree. A push that also carries a branch takes that branch's tier.
Until this hook reaches `main`, a hook installed from an older `main` still runs
the gates on a note push, and the push fails outside `nix develop`.

## The cap

At most `--max-rounds` rounds (default 5). When the last allowed round ends with
findings or blocked pieces, the script records the note, prints the open
findings, and exits 3. Asking for another round exits 3 without calling the
reviewer. Do not work around the cap: split the branch into smaller branches or
escalate to a human reviewer, and pass on the open findings.

The owner may then approve one exact commit (R-0618), after Codex, Kimi and agy have all reviewed
that commit. The cap is the class of the branch's diff against its base, by path, the highest class
present winning: docs and roadmap 2 rounds, scripts 3, compiler and spec 5 (an unclassified path
counts as compiler and spec); it counts the rounds used, whatever their verdicts. The approval is
recorded, from the owner's own words only, with `scripts/land/signoff.sh record <sha> --base <base>
--rounds-at <the review.sh-noted commit> --date --quote`, plus `--codex-request <request> --codex
<output>` and the corresponding kimi and agy options. For each reviewer `r`, the recorder stores the
verbatim request as `r-request.txt` after prepending `reviewed: <sha>`, and the output separately as
`r.txt`. The request body must name that commit by an unambiguous prefix, and only the output's last
verdict line counts; it must be `VERDICT: ACCEPT`. Recording refuses a record that does not qualify.
Push the resulting `refs/owner-signoff/<sha>` to origin. The landing train accepts it as that
commit's review provenance ([LANDING.md](LANDING.md)); it approves no other commit.

## verify.sh

```sh
scripts/review/verify.sh <commit>          # exit 0 clean, 1 findings/blocked, 2 none or malformed
scripts/review/verify.sh --fetch <commit>  # fetch the notes ref from origin first
```

It judges the exact commit. A clean note on an ancestor says nothing about the
commits after it, so a branch that gains a commit after its clean round needs
another round.

## Tests

`scripts/checks/review_tool.sh` runs the scripts against throwaway
repositories, with a bare repository as `origin` and a fake `codex` that answers
according to markers in the diff. It needs no API key. It covers delta selection,
splitting, the refusal retry, the cap, note writing and pushing (including the
non-fast-forward merge), verdict parsing, and `verify.sh`.
runs them here, says so, and logs `impact here`.

**Reuse at the `ci` push.** The root hook (`.githooks/pre-push`) writes no validation record and
never consults an attestation, so at the root no push skips its checks. What follows describes the
mechanism for a hook that writes one (the archived legacy hook): when the host's hook wrote a validation record, the log carries it
between `=== concrete-remote-record-begin` / `-end` markers. On a pass, the train worktree keeps two
files in its git directory:

- the log, at `concrete-prepush-remote-record.log`;
- an attestation, at `concrete-prepush-remote-record`.

The attestation records the head, the host, the log's digest and `complete=1`.

The pre-push hook stays the enforcement point. It skips its own run **only** when all of these hold:

- the attestation is complete and names this HEAD;
- the log's digest matches the one attested;
- the log shows exit 0 at this SHA;
- the embedded record is complete and a pass;
- every host-independent component equals this push's. These are the commit and every pushed
  ref, `lean-toolchain`, the committed gate definitions, the resolved selection, the changed-file
  list, the fuzz seed and the `CONCRETE_*` environment. The tracked tree must also be clean.

Host-specific components (compiler binary, lean, cc) describe the host and are not compared. If any
check fails, the hook names the reason and runs the gates here. If the host passed but wrote no
record, the leg passes and says that the push will run the gates locally.

Each run of `gates_remote.sh`, and each `check`, first removes the previous attestation. The attestation and its log are files in the git
directory, like the local record. They catch mismatches, truncation and digest-breaking edits. They
are not a signature against someone who can write the git directory.

## Where the record lives (R-0547)

Every subcommand appends one row to the train's log:

- the time in UTC;
- the train, stage, base and head;
- the members, as `ref@sha`;
- the run id, result and duration;
- a note.

Results use the promotion pipeline's vocabulary:

- `passed`, `failed` and `skipped`;
- `incomplete` for no verdict;
- `refused` and `conflict` for a refusal by the script.

**Decision: the log is kept local, one file per train, in the git common directory**
(`<git-common-dir>/concrete-landing/log/<train>.tsv`). Train state lives next to it, in
`<train>/train.env`. The reasons:

- **Committing it to the train changes the candidate.** A row appended to the train after CI ran
  would land a SHA that CI never validated. The `land` row cannot exist before the land happens.
- **One shared TSV conflicts.** Every train would append to the same file, which recreates the
  ledger conflicts R-0542 removes. One file per train cannot conflict.
- **The common directory is shared by every worktree.** `report.sh` and `train.sh status` see every
  train of the clone from any of its worktrees; building, checking and landing run only in the full
  clone itself ("Where a train runs").

To publish the record, `report.sh --export docs/project/landing_log/` copies the per-train files
into the tree for a documentation-only commit. Per-train files never conflict on rebase. That
directory is what R-0547's generated median, next to the candidate table, will read.

## Readiness

A branch is **ready** when all of the following hold:

- it is on origin, named `wip/<name>`;
- its head commit is not in main;
- its head carries both notes below, published on origin.

| notes ref | lines | writer |
|---|---|---|
| `refs/notes/concrete-review` | `verdict: clean\|findings\|blocked`, `rounds: N`, `reviewer: codex`, `utc: ...`, `base: <sha>`, `pieces: N` | `scripts/review/review.sh` (`scripts/review/verify.sh` reads it) |
| `refs/notes/concrete-gates` | `gates: pass\|fail`, `selected: N`, `utc: ...` | `scripts/land/ready.sh` |

One `key: value` per line; when a key repeats, the last line wins. `scripts/land/notes.sh` reads and
writes the notes (`get`, `put`, `ready`). `put` builds each write on its own temporary ref on top
of the remote's notes tip, pushes that commit without force, and retries on a race. Concurrent
writers therefore never drop each other's notes, including writers that share one clone or its
worktrees.

**Decision: candidates are `wip/*` branches whose head carries both notes. There is no `ready/*`
prefix.** The reasons:

- **A note is bound to a commit.** A new push starts with no notes, so "ready at the exact head"
  needs no extra rule, and no ready branch can go stale.
- **Agents already push `wip/*`.** The pre-push hook's backup tier keeps those pushes cheap, and
  marking a branch ready is a metadata write, not another branch to keep in step.
- **The ready list is a query**, so nothing has to be cleaned up after a landing.

`wip/land-*` is reserved for the remote leg's temporary refs and is never a candidate.

To mark a branch ready, push it, then run `scripts/land/ready.sh wip/<name>` from a clean
checkout at its head. It runs the review, the impact gates and the remote full set in parallel,
then publishes the gates note: `gates: pass` only when every leg that ran passed. A failed review
leg also publishes `gates: fail`, so an older clean verdict cannot keep the head ready. It exits 0
when the head is ready.

## Pipelining

The review, the impact gates and the remote full set do not depend on each other. `ready.sh` and
`train.sh check` therefore start all three together and wait for all of them. The wall time
becomes the slowest leg instead of the sum. Both scripts record each leg's duration, the wall time
and the sum: `ready.sh` in `<git-common-dir>/concrete-landing/ready.tsv`, and `check` as the
`check-legs` row of the train log.

**Measured on 2026-10-22, on branch `wip/landing-bot`.** Two runs of `ready.sh`, each with its legs
started together:

| run | review (Codex) | impact | remote full set | wall | one after another |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| laptop, `f55935ac` | 117 s | 51 s | lost when the laptop lost its network | n/a | n/a |
| concrete-ci-1, `93aa7ac5` | not run (no Codex login for `dev`) | 69 s | 5239 s, red | 5239 s | 5308 s |

The server was shared with other full runs at a load average of 15 to 32.

- **Saving.** The impact gates (69 s) and the review (117 s, from the laptop run) finish inside the
  remote leg, so about 3 minutes come off every readiness check. Measured: 69 s with the impact
  leg alone, and 186 s with the review included.
- **What dominates.** The remote full set is about 98% of the cost: 87 minutes on a loaded host.
  Pipelining removes the queue in front of it, not the leg itself. The next lever is the full
  set's own time (R-0546 caches, R-0541 dedicated runners) and keeping concurrent full runs off
  the same host.
- **Why the remote leg was red.** Five gates failed:
  - `check_phase6c_observability.sh` #5 and `check_campaign_summary_golden.sh` refuse to run in a
    linked worktree ("Run it in a clone"), which is how that run was made. `run-at-ref.sh` now
    uses `clone --local`.
  - `check_example_outputs.sh` under valgrind, `run_tests.sh` and `check_external_checker.sh`
    were not examined.

  This branch changes only `scripts/land`, one build-free gate with its registration, and this
  document. CI on a train is where those three would be settled.
- **Not counted.** Before pipelining, a human orchestrator usually started the review, the impact
  gates and the remote set by hand in sequence, and each hand-off waited for someone to notice
  the previous result. The scripts remove that wait, and this table does not measure it.

## Configuration
| variable | default | meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `LAND_REPO` | `lambdaclass/concrete` | repository for `gh` |
| `LAND_REMOTE` | `origin` | the one repository's remote |
| `LAND_CAMPAIGN_JOBS` | `^(Gate mutation\|Weekly clean-checkout proof replay\|Nightly differential fuzz\|Checker pin freshness \(campaign finding\))` | job names in the campaign tier; the default must match exactly the workflow's campaign jobs (`check_landing_script.sh`) |
| `LAND_STATE_DIR`, `LAND_LOG_DIR` | under the git common directory | state and log location |
| `LAND_NO_NIX` | unset | `1` never re-enters `nix develop` |
| `LAND_GH`, `LAND_HOOK` | `gh`, `.githooks/pre-push` | test seams |
| `LAND_GATES_HOST` | `git config land.gatesHost` | gate host (ssh destination) for `check`'s impact leg; empty = none; `check --local-gates` runs here instead |
| `LAND_GATES_TRANSPORT` | `scripts/land/gates_transport_ssh.sh` | `<transport> <host> <bundle> <ref> <command>`, printing `run-at-ref.sh`'s output; `LAND_GATES_SSH`/`LAND_GATES_SCP` override its ssh and scp |
| `LAND_REMOTE_RUNNER` | `~/bin/concrete-remote` | `<runner> <ref> <command>` for the remote leg; empty disables it |
| `LAND_REMOTE_PUSH` | `1` | `0`: the runner fetches the SHA from this clone, nothing is published |
| `LAND_FULL_GATES_CMD` | `lake build && bash scripts/checks/run.sh` | the remote leg's command |
| `LAND_REVIEW_CMD` | `scripts/review/review.sh` when present | the review leg's command (`--base --head` appended); with neither, `check` fails |
| `LAND_PUSH_ATTEMPTS`, `LAND_PUSH_RETRY_SLEEP` | `3`, `15` | attempts and pause for a verified push |
## Tests

`scripts/checks/landing_script.sh` runs in CI's build-free grammar job. It drives the real
script against a throwaway repository with bare-repo remotes and a fake `gh`. It covers:

- every resolution route;
- every refusal: a real conflict, prose next to a generated block, a duplicate corpus key, a merge
  or rebase already in progress, a dirty tree, an existing train, a head-SHA mismatch, a red
  landing job, a moved main and a remote named `fork`;
- the check → ci → watch → land pipeline, with both dry-runs;
- one repository: with no `lambdaclass` remote configured, `land` succeeds and its exact push log is
  one push to origin; `ci` defaults `LAND_REPO` to lambdaclass/concrete; `push-both.sh` makes exactly
  one push and reports its destination;
- `ci` dispatches with the train's base; `land` cancels no run; `record` names the qualifying run
  only for the newest completed dispatch of CI at exactly the head and the base with every
  landing-tier and required job green, and refuses each of: no run, a run at another SHA or base, a
  push, pull-request or base-less dispatch, another workflow, an unfinished run or the asking run
  itself, a skipped, cancelled or failed job, an older green run behind a newer red one, a head not
  strictly ahead of the base, an absent or all-zero base and unreadable APIs; and the workflow gates
  every landing-tier job on the record and every base-sensitive check on the dispatch's base (with
  controls for an ungated job, a dependent forced by `always()`, a gate without `!cancelled()` and
  a check that ignores the base);
- verified pushes: a push dropped before the remote moved is retried, one dropped after it moved is
  accepted from `ls-remote`, and exhausted retries fail without moving main;
- the gate host: a result bound to the train head's SHA. A run at another SHA, an unreachable
  host and a lost log are each INCOMPLETE and block. A failure at the head fails. `--local-gates`
  and `land.gatesHost` work as described. `run-at-ref.sh` runs a bundled head. The hook's library
  reuses an attestation only for this exact push and refuses each of the following: another SHA,
  changed files, selection or environment, a dirty tree, a tampered or re-digested log, a tampered
  or truncated attestation, and a log that is not a pass;
- bisection;
- the report.

`scripts/checks/landing_ready.sh` runs in the same job. It drives the real `ready.sh` and
`train.sh` against a throwaway repository with bare-repo remotes, a fake `gh`, a fake remote runner
and a fake hook that fails exactly when the checkout contains `BAD`. It covers:

- the remote leg: an existing ref at the candidate's SHA is refused, not adopted; the runner runs
  once, on a fresh `wip/land-*` ref, and only the run's own ref is deleted;
- `bisect --ci`: a member whose CI is red is named, each probe dispatching its own run, and nothing
  lands;
- `ready.sh`: a failed review leg publishing `gates: fail` over an older clean verdict, and
  passing legs making a head ready;
- a static check that nothing under `scripts/land` force-pushes main.
# Self-hosted CI runners

The CI workflow (`.github/workflows/lean_action_ci.yml`) and the branch-health workflow can run
their Ubuntu jobs on our own servers instead of GitHub-hosted `ubuntu-latest`. This page covers what
the switch does, how to set up a server, and the security rules for a **public** repository.

## What the switch does

Every Ubuntu job's `runs-on` is:

```yaml
runs-on: ${{ (github.event_name != 'pull_request' && vars.CONCRETE_SELF_HOSTED == 'true') && fromJSON('["self-hosted","linux","x64","concrete"]') || 'ubuntu-latest' }}
```

- The repository variable `CONCRETE_SELF_HOSTED` routes the **landing tier** (build, test suites,
  trust, proof and evidence jobs, the language-surface jobs, multi-kernel) and branch health.
- `CONCRETE_SELF_HOSTED_CAMPAIGN` routes the **campaign tier** (mutation plan, the 24 mutation
  shards, the aggregate, the fuzz campaign, the proof replay) on its own, and the two light jobs of
  the nightly workflow (`nightly.yml`: the dispatch and the report, which need `gh` and `python3`). Leave it unset
  unless the servers can absorb 24 shards of up to ~85 minutes each. Labels give no priority, so a
  campaign on self-hosted runners would queue landing runs behind it.
  The tiers are listed in [PROMOTION_PIPELINE.md](../legacy/docs/project/PROMOTION_PIPELINE.md#ci-tiers).
- An unset variable, or any value other than `true`, means GitHub-hosted. That is the default.
- **A `pull_request` event never runs on a self-hosted runner**, whatever the variables say. See
  the security section for why this guard alone is not enough.
- `Build + main tests (macOS)` stays on `macos-latest`. The Pages workflow (`book.yml`) stays on
  GitHub-hosted runners.

**Two pools.** CI's jobs ask for the label `concrete`; branch health asks for `concrete-light`, a
separate pool of small runners (two on concrete-ci-2, `LIGHT_RUNNERS` in the dotfiles host file).
Branch health runs on every push to every branch, about 500 runs a day, and on the `concrete` pool
those runs took 20-92 runner-minutes out of each train's window (measured 2026-10-24). Labels give
no priority, so the only way to keep a train from queueing behind them is to not share the pool.
A `concrete-light` runner carries only that label, so CI never lands on it either.

### Runner pools and their limits (P13, owner-approved 2026-11-05)

CI runs on two regular pools: concrete-ci-2's self-hosted runners and GitHub-hosted runners. Neither
is a fallback that weakens a check; each required check runs on a pool that can run it, or is
INCOMPLETE and blocks the landing.

| Pool | Where | Limits (set 2026-11-06) | Runs |
|---|---|---|---|
| `concrete` | concrete-ci-2 (Hetzner AX102: 32 threads, 125 GB RAM) | 6 runners `gha1`..`gha6`, so at most 6 concurrent jobs; each has `MemoryMax=28G` and no swap, and no per-runner CPU cap | the landing tier when `CONCRETE_SELF_HOSTED=true`; never a `pull_request` |
| `concrete-light` | concrete-ci-2 | 2 runners `gha7`, `gha8` (`LIGHT_RUNNERS` in the dotfiles host file), each `CPUQuota=400%` and `MemoryMax=8G` | branch health only |
| GitHub-hosted | `ubuntu-latest`, `macos-latest` | GitHub's per-repository concurrency | every landing-tier job when the variable is off; always: `Oracle (GitHub-hosted fetch + smoke)` and `Build + main tests (macOS)`; every `pull_request` |

Both pools on concrete-ci-2 run inside `concrete-runners.slice`, capped together at `CPUQuota=2400%`
(24 of 32 threads), `MemoryMax=96G`, no swap and `TasksMax=16384` (ISS-0007, applied 2026-11-06).
The runners together can therefore never use more than 24 CPU-equivalents or 96 GB, which leaves
8 CPU-equivalents and about 25 GB of headroom for gate runs, agents and logins in `user.slice`. This is
headroom, not a reservation: those processes share it, and only `system.slice`'s 4 GB `MemoryMin` (sshd,
tailscaled) is reserved. The slice keeps the default CPU weight (100) against
`user.slice`'s 25, so CI still wins under contention. There is no `MemoryHigh`: a job past its
`MemoryMax` is killed inside the slice rather than throttled. The values live in the dotfiles host
file (`servers/hosts/concrete-ci-2.env`, `CONCRETE_RUNNERS_*` and `CONCRETE_*RUNNER_*`), and
`servers/runners.sh` writes them, so a rebuilt host gets the same caps.

Manual heavy work on concrete-ci-2 (train check legs through `land.gatesHost`, `run-at-ref.sh`
runs, oracle rebuilds) follows the machine-wide rule: at most two full suites, mutation campaigns
or other CPU-heavy checks at once (AGENTS.md, ROADMAP R-0543).

**What runs where on the GitHub-hosted pool.** Every landing-tier job's `runs-on` falls back to
`ubuntu-latest`, so the whole landing tier can run there; the only self-hosted-only steps are cache
seeding (`Seed .lake from the server build cache`), whose absence means a cold build, never a
skipped check. The oracle differential gets its oracle on a GitHub-hosted runner from the published
release (`scripts/oracle/fetch.sh`: the lock's artifact sha256, install manifest and binary hash);
`scripts/oracle/ci_check.sh` reports a missing, corrupted or unverifiable artifact as INCOMPLETE
(exit 3), which fails the required job (`scripts/checks/oracle_fetch.sh` holds this). The
oracle rebuild (`oracle.yml`, weekly) runs on either pool.

A job routed to self-hosted waits until a runner with **all four** labels `self-hosted`, `linux`,
`x64` and its pool label (`concrete`, or `concrete-light` for branch health) is idle. If no runner is online, it waits (up to 24 hours) rather than falling
back to GitHub-hosted. So turn the variable off before taking the servers down.

### Switching it on and off

```sh
# on (landing tier)
gh variable set CONCRETE_SELF_HOSTED --body true -R lambdaclass/concrete
# off (back to GitHub-hosted)
gh variable delete CONCRETE_SELF_HOSTED -R lambdaclass/concrete
# campaign tier, separately (normally left off)
gh variable set CONCRETE_SELF_HOSTED_CAMPAIGN --body true -R lambdaclass/concrete
```

The variable is read when a run starts. Runs already queued keep the routing they started with.

**One repository.** Since 2026-11-06 the runners serve lambdaclass/concrete only
([decision](decisions.md)); concrete2, where they were registered until
then, is archived once a full landing has run on lambdaclass/concrete. Variables and repository-level runners belong to one repository: the move
re-registered each runner there (the dotfiles `servers/runners.sh`, `RUNNER_REINSTALL=1`, which
reinstalls the runner into a fresh user), and `CONCRETE_SELF_HOSTED=true` is set on
lambdaclass/concrete. The root has no nightly campaign yet; `CONCRETE_SELF_HOSTED_CAMPAIGN` matters
only when one returns.

## Security rules (public repository)

GitHub's own guidance is that self-hosted runners should not be used with public repositories,
because a pull request can run arbitrary code on them. We use them anyway, under these rules:

**Who can reach the runners at all** (owner, 2026-11-06, "A";
[decisions](decisions.md)). On lambdaclass/concrete every org member
has push: 44 accounts. A push of any branch, or a `workflow_dispatch`, runs the workflow file of that
branch, so any of them can put a job on these runners with no approval; the rules below do not
change that. The owner accepted this exposure, as for stabileo's runners. On concrete2 one account
had push.

1. **No `pull_request` event runs on a self-hosted runner.** The `runs-on` expression enforces this
   for the workflow as written. But a pull request runs the workflow file **from the PR branch**, so
   a fork PR can rewrite `runs-on` to `[self-hosted]` and the guard is gone. The guard prevents
   accidents. It does not stop an attacker. Rules 2 and 3 do.
2. **Require approval for all outside collaborators.** In Settings, Actions, General, "Approval for
   running fork pull request workflows from contributors", choose "Require approval for all outside
   collaborators". Before approving a fork PR's run, read its diff to `.github/workflows/`. A
   changed `runs-on`, a new job, or a new trigger is a reason not to approve.
3. **Ephemeral runners in throwaway VMs or containers are recommended.** `./config.sh ...
   --ephemeral` only makes the runner take one job and then unregister. **It does not erase
   anything:** files, surviving processes, cron entries, a modified `~/.bashrc` or toolchain all
   stay on the machine. Isolation comes from destroying the VM or container after each job and
   starting a fresh one from a clean image, which registers a new ephemeral runner. Automate that
   with actions-runner-controller, or with a supervisor **outside** the job's VM or container that
   calls `gh api -X POST repos/lambdaclass/concrete/actions/runners/registration-token`.
   The supervisor's admin token must never be readable from inside a job. Persistent runners (or
   `--ephemeral` re-registered on the same machine, which is the same thing) are acceptable only
   with rule 2, the cleanup hook below and a dedicated low-privilege user, and with the
   understanding that one job can leave state for the next.
4. **Dedicated, unprivileged users and hosts.** Use one Unix user per runner, with no SSH keys,
   cloud credentials or other repositories' secrets readable, and **no sudo**. Don't run other
   services on the host.
5. The triggers that do reach self-hosted runners are `push` to `main`, `push` to other branches
   (branch health), `workflow_dispatch` and `schedule`. All of them need write access to the
   repository. The nightly report job is a `workflow_run` of CI that runs only for a
   `workflow_dispatch` run on `main` (never for a pull request's run) and checks out the default
   branch, so no pull request's code reaches it.

## Server setup

### Host

- Ubuntu 24.04 x86-64, the same image family as `ubuntu-latest`. The `.lake/build` and `~/.elan`
  caches are keyed by `runner.os`/`runner.arch` and shared with GitHub-hosted runs. Lake re-checks
  its traces, so a mismatched cache rebuilds instead of being trusted. But a different distribution
  makes every self-hosted build cold.
- **One runner per ~4 cores**, with at least 8 GB RAM and 30 GB disk each. The Lean build and the
  heavier gates are memory-bound, and a runner that swaps is slower than a GitHub-hosted one. For
  example, a 16-core, 64 GB server takes 4 runners.

### Packages to preinstall

The workflow installs `clang` (and `valgrind` for the examples job) with `sudo apt-get` on
GitHub-hosted runners. On a self-hosted runner it does so **only when the tool is missing**, and the
runner users have no sudo (see below). So preinstall everything:

```sh
sudo apt-get update
sudo apt-get install -y git curl ca-certificates build-essential clang valgrind \
  python3 python3-pip python3-yaml xxd perl tar zstd ripgrep coreutils gh
# Rust (rustc + cargo), per runner user, like the stable toolchain on GitHub-hosted images:
sudo -iu runner1 sh -c 'curl --proto =https --tlsv1.2 -sSf https://sh.rustup.rs | sh -s -- -y --profile minimal'
```

- `rustc` is used by the LL(1) grammar job. `cargo` builds nanoda for the external-checker job
  whenever `~/.cache/concrete-ext` has no build for the pinned SHA. Ubuntu's `rustc` package does
  not pull in `cargo`, so use rustup (or install both `rustc` and `cargo`), and check it once by
  running the external-checker job with an empty `~/.cache/concrete-ext`. Rustup puts its tools in
  `~/.cargo/bin`, so install it before `./config.sh`, or add that directory to the runner's `.path`.
- `python3-yaml` is used by the workflow, example and qualification gates, `xxd` and `curl` by
  the workload differentials, and `zstd` by `actions/cache`. `gh` (GitHub CLI; Ubuntu 24.04 ships
  it as the `gh` package) is used by the nightly workflow's dispatch and report jobs, which run on
  these runners under `CONCRETE_SELF_HOSTED_CAMPAIGN`.
- **Lean (elan) is not preinstalled.** `leanprover/lean-action` installs elan into `~/.elan` on
  first use, and the toolchain stays there: the runner user's home persists between jobs. The
  `~/.elan` cache step runs on GitHub-hosted runners only. On these runners it re-downloaded a
  2.3 GB archive of toolchains the home already held, 2-4 minutes per job and about a third of all
  runner time in a train (measured 2026-10-23). A toolchain bump is installed once per runner by
  lean-action (and the 4.35 leg by its `elan toolchain install` step). With one user per runner,
  each runner has its own `~/.elan`, so concurrent jobs never race on one toolchain directory.
- **Nix** is needed by exactly one job, `Multi-kernel evidence (Rocq + Isabelle)`, which runs
  `nix develop .#provers`. Install multi-user Nix (daemon) **before** configuring the runner, so
  `/nix/var/nix/profiles/default/bin` is on the PATH the runner records in its `.path` file. Enable
  flakes in `/etc/nix/nix.conf` (`experimental-features = nix-command flakes`).
  `cachix/install-nix-action` sees an existing `nix` and skips its own install, so its
  `extra_nix_config` is **not** applied: the system config must carry the flakes setting. The
  provers closure is several GB; a persistent `/nix/store` makes this job much faster than on
  GitHub-hosted runners.
- **Do not install** `z3`, `cvc5`, `coqc`, `isabelle` or `lli` system-wide. Several gates
  treat those as skip-if-absent, so installing them makes a self-hosted run execute legs that the
  GitHub-hosted reference run skips, and the two runs no longer agree. The provers come from the
  Nix shell in the one job that uses them.
- **No sudo.** On a self-hosted runner, the `Install clang` steps run `sudo apt-get` only when
  the tool is missing. Without sudo, that step fails and names the problem, which is the intended
  result: install the package on the host. Do **not** grant passwordless `sudo apt-get`.
  Unrestricted apt arguments give root (apt hooks, `-o` options, local packages), and that defeats
  the per-runner user boundary.

### Installing a runner

Do this once per runner, each in its own user (`runner1`, `runner2`, ...) and its own directory:

```sh
sudo useradd -m -s /bin/bash runner1
sudo -iu runner1
mkdir actions-runner && cd actions-runner
# Take the current version and checksum from Settings, Actions, Runners, New self-hosted runner.
curl -o actions-runner.tar.gz -L https://github.com/actions/runner/releases/download/v<VERSION>/actions-runner-linux-x64-<VERSION>.tar.gz
tar xzf actions-runner.tar.gz
# The token is shown on that settings page (or: gh api -X POST repos/lambdaclass/concrete/actions/runners/registration-token)
./config.sh --url https://github.com/lambdaclass/concrete --token <TOKEN> \
  --name "$(hostname)-runner1" --labels concrete --unattended   # add --ephemeral (recommended)

exec

docs/decisions.md:1292:| OD2 | <a id="od2"></a>`&mut` arguments: an implicit reborrow; overlapping exclusive arguments refused; a deferred call keeps its loan until it runs; one defined order for cleanup, loan end and write-back | D1 and §4.5; the reborrow implemented in landing train 6 (REF-10); the defer and exit-order rules not yet | exit 2i (G9, B1, B2), so every deliverable; R-0496 (Core reborrow form) | decided 2026-10-29 by the owner: (1) passing a `&mut` on is plain lending, each call's use ending when it returns, with no reborrow concept or syntax; (2) a call is refused when an exclusive argument overlaps any other argument (`h(r, r)`, `h(&mut x, &x)`, `h(&mut a, &mut a.f)` refused); as tightened by OD15 (a) the same day, two arguments that borrow the same root conflict whenever one is exclusive, so even `h(&mut a.f, &mut a.g)` is refused; (3) `&mut` refers to the place, a write through it is the write to that place at once, and copy-in/copy-out is not part of the language (only an optimization proven equivalent); (4) `defer` keeps OWN-12 and a borrow passed to a deferred call stays live until the call runs; (5) one exit sequence on every non-abort exit: the exit value, then the scope's deferred calls (last registered first, innermost scope first), then the scope's loans end, then the remaining locals are destroyed; an abort runs none of it **Canonical current root-based exclusivity rule: REF-11 and OD15(a); this historical overlap formulation is a reference to that rule.** |

docs/decisions.md:1397:| OD107 | <a id="od107"></a>What a float-to-integer rounding gives for NaN, infinities and out-of-range values (added 2026-10-30, from the Codex and Kimi review of the absorption). Bears on TYPE-D1, TYPE-17, TYPE-4. docs/decisions.md:1399:| OD109 | <a id="od109"></a>Specializations that differ only in a function type's capabilities (added 2026-10-30, from the Codex and Kimi review of the absorption). Bears on TYPE-17, TYPE-18. docs/decisions.md:1401:| OD111 | <a id="od111"></a>Misaligned fields of a packed struct (added 2026-10-30, from the Codex and Kimi review of the absorption). Question: Whether a `#[repr(packed)]` struct may hold a field whose alignment exceeds the alignment of its offset, and whether such a field may be borrowed. Bears on FFI-4, FFI-8, REF-5. Question: Whether a value reached through a shared reference `&T` may be changed, by a write through a raw pointer derived from the reference or by a cell type. Bears on REF-10, OWN-10, TRUST-2, TRUST-5. The current compiler: safe code changes a value only through a `mut` binding or a `&mut` reference, and there is no cell type; a trusted function may convert `r: &Int` to `*mut Int` (`r as *const Int as *mut Int`) and store through it: the binary does the store, and `--interp` stops ("deref-assign through immutable ref"). docs/decisions.md:1403:| OD113 | <a id="od113"></a>The ownership exceptions of trusted code (added 2026-10-30, from the Codex and Kimi review of the absorption). Question: Whether trusted code keeps its two exceptions to the ownership rules, destroying a value through a reference and releasing with `vec_free` a vector whose elements are not Copy. Bears on TRUST-5, TRUST-12, OWN-5, OWN-8. The current compiler: both are accepted in trusted code only (elsewhere E0290 under OWN-8 and E0287 under OWN-5): std's collections destroy an element through `(p as &T)` in drop glue after its owner was consumed, and a trusted function may free a buffer it has emptied. The archived [MEMORY_SEMANTICS.md](../legacy/docs/archive/design-records/MEMORY_SEMANTICS.md) §11 said that `trusted` does not relax linearity, and TRUST-5's rationale says the same. docs/decisions.md:1404:| OD114 | <a id="od114"></a>How integer division rounds (added 2026-10-30, from the Codex and Kimi review of the absorption). Question: How an integer division that does not abort rounds its quotient, and which sign a nonzero remainder takes. Bears on ARITH-3. The current compiler: the quotient is truncated toward zero and a nonzero remainder has the sign of the dividend, as C99 and LLVM's `sdiv` and `srem` give (`-7 / 2` is `-3`, `-7 % 2` is `-1`, `7 % -2` is `1`), in the binary, the interpreter and the constant folder (`IntArith.evalIntBinOp`). The archived [ARITHMETIC_POLICY.md](../legacy/docs/archive/design-records/ARITHMETIC_POLICY.md) states when division aborts (§5) but not how it rounds. Options: (a) truncation toward zero, as in the current compiler; (b) floored division, the remainder taking the divisor's sign; (c) Euclidean division, the remainder never negative. Austral: not checked. Pick: none given (raised by the Codex and Kimi review of the absorption, 2026-10-30). | the quotient is truncated toward zero and a remainder has the dividend's sign (C99) | ARITH-3 | decided 2026-10-31 by the owner's rule (every reviewer agreed, as strict as Austral or stricter): Truncation toward zero |
docs/decisions.md:1418:| OD128 | <a id="od128"></a>`->` between a match arm's pattern and body (added 2026-10-30, raised while absorbing the compiler documents and the grammar comments). Question: Whether a match arm may separate its pattern from its body with `->` as well as `=>`. Bears on SYN-1 and PAT-7. The current compiler: the parser accepts both (`parseMatchArms`; `2 -> { return 42; }` exits 42 on both consumers), and the grammar's `match_arm` has only `=>`. The syntax freeze review (2026-04, [SYNTAX_FREEZE_REVIEW.md](../legacy/docs/archive/design-records/SYNTAX_FREEZE_REVIEW.md) §1.5) recommended `=>` as the canonical form with a warning for `->`, which also spells a return type. Options: (a) `=>` only, `->` refused; (b) both accepted; (c) both accepted, with a warning on `->`, as the freeze review recommended. Austral: not applicable. Pick: none given (raised by the absorption of docs/compiler, 2026-10-30). | see OPEN-128 | see OPEN-128 | decided 2026-11-01 by the owner (final set): `=>` only |
docs/decisions.md:1462:  lists its real capabilities; std, examples and tests are to migrate under exit 2q, with the compiler
exit
cd /home/runner1/actions-runner
sudo ./svc.sh install runner1
sudo ./svc.sh start
```

`self-hosted`, `linux` and `x64` are added automatically, and `--labels concrete` adds the fourth
label the workflow asks for.

### State between jobs (persistent runners)

A GitHub-hosted job starts on a fresh VM. A self-hosted one does not. Here is what the workflow
already handles, and what the host has to handle.

- **Workspace.** `actions/checkout@v4` (default `clean: true`) runs `git clean -ffdx` and
  `git reset --hard` on the existing checkout. That removes `.lake/`, `.mutation-evidence/`,
  leftovers of a cancelled mutation shard and any tracked file a gate mutated, before the cache
  restores `.lake/build`. The proof replay's "no build caches" property holds for the same
  reason.
- **`$RUNNER_TEMP`** is emptied by the runner for every job. The workflow's own temporary files
  live there: the LL(1) checker binaries, the trust-gate log, the thesis-demo reports, the example
  outputs, the checker acceptance evidence and the mutation shard records.
- **`/tmp` is NOT cleaned, and many gates write fixed `/tmp/...` paths.** Two runners on one host
  would overwrite each other's files, and a stale file from an earlier job could be read by a later
  one. Give every runner service a private `/tmp`, and empty it when each job starts:

  ```sh
  # Private /tmp per runner service (systemd unit created by svc.sh)
  sudo systemctl edit "$(cat /home/runner1/actions-runner/.service)"
  #   [Service]
  #   PrivateTmp=true
  sudo systemctl restart "$(cat /home/runner1/actions-runner/.service)"
  ```

  ```sh
  # /home/runner1/job-started.sh  (chmod +x), wired up in the runner's .env file:
  #   ACTIONS_RUNNER_HOOK_JOB_STARTED=/home/runner1/job-started.sh
  #!/bin/sh
  # /tmp is private to this runner service (PrivateTmp), so emptying it touches no other process.
  find /tmp -mindepth 1 -maxdepth 1 -user "$(id -u)" -exec rm -rf {} + 2>/dev/null || true
  ```

  An ephemeral runner in a fresh container or VM needs neither.
- **`$HOME` caches** persist: `~/.elan` (the toolchain, keyed by `lean-toolchain`) and
  `~/.cache/concrete-ext`. In that cache, each tool is built into a directory named by its pinned
  SHA and checked against it, and lean4export, which is rebuilt with this repository's toolchain,
  also has that toolchain in its directory name. So a toolchain bump builds a new exporter instead of
  reusing one that cannot read the new `.olean` files. Both caches are keyed by everything their
  builds depend on, so keeping them between jobs makes runs faster without affecting correctness.
  They live in the user's HOME, so each runner has its own. Old directories are never deleted; prune
  `~/.cache/concrete-ext` now and then.
- **Processes.** The runner kills a job's process tree when the job ends. A gate that leaves a
  daemon behind would survive on a persistent runner. That is one more reason to prefer ephemeral
  runners.

### Checking it works

1. Settings, Actions, Runners lists each runner as Idle, with labels
   `self-hosted, Linux, X64, concrete`.
2. `gh variable set CONCRETE_SELF_HOSTED --body true -R lambdaclass/concrete`.
3. `gh workflow run CI --ref main -R lambdaclass/concrete` (landing tier only), then
   check in the run's job pages that "Runner name" is one of yours and that `Install clang` printed
   `self-hosted runner, using preinstalled clang`.
4. To turn it off, delete the variable. Queued jobs that are still waiting for a self-hosted
   runner have to be cancelled and re-run.

## Build cache (R-0546)

Every job, worktree and agent used to build the compiler from nothing. Two caches now start that
build warm. **Neither one skips it.** `lake build` still runs and re-checks every module's trace,
and the BuildIdentity freshness gate still runs.

### Lean: `.lake/build` seeds

`scripts/dev/seed-lake.sh` copies an existing `.lake/build` into a checkout that has none. It
**refuses** (exit 3, nothing copied) unless the source matches on the **seed key**:

- the platform (`uname -s`/`-m`);
- `lean-toolchain`;
- the sha256 of `lake-manifest.json`;
- the sha256 of the **lean executable itself**.

The executable is in the key because lake's traces record only the Lean version and commit. Elan's
official archive and the devshell's autoPatchelf'd Linux copy would otherwise accept each other's
`.olean` files, and a module kernel-checked by one binary would be credited to the other (R-0208).
For a checkout, the script reads the binary from the checkout's own lake traces. Traces from other
Lean versions are ignored, because lake rebuilds those modules anyway.

Sources and `lakefile.toml` are not in the key. A seed from other sources is the useful case, and
lake mixes each module's sources and options into its trace. A source hash only chooses between
cache entries.

| Where | Seed source | How |
|---|---|---|
| Self-hosted CI runner | `/var/cache/concrete-lake/<key>/{<srchash>,latest}` | workflow step `Seed .lake from the server build cache (self-hosted only)` |
| GitHub-hosted runner | `actions/cache` (`.lake/build`, exact source key) and lean-action's `.lake` cache (previous commit) | unchanged |
| New worktree (laptop or server) | the main checkout's `.lake/build` | `bash scripts/dev/seed-lake.sh` (inside `nix develop`), then `lake build` |

- **Every same-version module must name its executable.** A trace from this Lean version that
  does not name the binary that compiled it refuses the whole seed, because it may be a fetched
  artifact or an unknown log format. The check assumes that the binary at a recorded path has not
  been replaced in place. That holds for Nix store paths, which are immutable, and for elan
  toolchains, which use one directory per release.
- **A consistent snapshot.** Lake takes no lock that the seeder could share. A checkout source must
  therefore be quiet: nothing in it may be written from 10 s before the copy
  (`CONCRETE_SEED_QUIET`) until the copy ends. A `lake build` that is running, or has just
  finished, in the main checkout refuses the seed. Provenance is checked again on the copy itself.
  So is the order of writes: an `.olean` or `.ilean` newer than its trace means the build was
  killed or suspended mid-module, and that refuses the seed too. One risk remains and is accepted:
  checkout files edited by hand with matching timestamps are not detected. CI seeds only from
  published entries.
  Published cache entries need neither check. Each one is moved into place whole, after a completed
  build, and never written again. One seeder runs per checkout at a time (`.lake/seed.lock`).
- **CI checks against `~/.elan`'s lean** (`--elan`). That is the binary lean-action builds with,
  whatever else is on `PATH`.
- **Copies, never hardlinks.** Lake rewrites trace files in place, so a hardlink would write through
  into the seed. macOS uses APFS clones (`cp -c`, instant, no extra space). The server uses a plain
  copy, about 1 s for 600 MB.
- In CI, a seeded job sets `use-github-cache: false` on lean-action. Otherwise lean-action would
  restore an older commit's `.lake` over the seed. An exact-hit of the `.lake/build`
  `actions/cache` step comes first. The seed then refuses to overwrite it, which is the right
  outcome, because the exact hit is the better start.
- `--build [args]` seeds and then always `exec`s `lake build args`. The exit status is the build's.
- `scripts/tests/check_build_cache.sh` gates the key, the refusals, the copy-not-link rule,
  publish/consume, and the workflow wiring.

**The cache producer on concrete-ci-1.** The system user `lakecache` (no login, no sudo, home
`/var/lib/concrete-lake`, its own elan) owns `/var/cache/concrete-lake`. That directory is
world-readable and writable only by `lakecache`. `concrete-lake-refresh.timer` runs every 10
minutes and starts `concrete-lake-refresh.service`, which runs at `Nice=15`, `CPUWeight=20` and
`IOWeight=20`, with `ProtectSystem=strict` and `ProtectHome=true`. The service:

1. fetches `origin/main` into `/var/lib/concrete-lake/main`, a persistent clone, so the build is
   incremental;
2. runs `/usr/local/lib/concrete-lake/seed-lake.sh --publish /var/cache/concrete-lake`, which runs
   `lake build` and publishes only if it succeeds.

The service keeps three entries per key and updates `latest` atomically. The installed copy of
`seed-lake.sh` must be updated by hand when the key format changes. The key starts with
`format=concrete-lake-seed-v1`, so a format change makes the old entries miss instead of match
wrongly.

**Why only `lakecache` writes.** Lake trusts a module's stored trace and `.hash` files. A job that
could write the seed could plant an `.olean` file whose trace still matches its sources, and later
jobs would use it without rebuilding. Runner users (`gha1..6`) and `dev` can only read the cache.
The producer builds only `origin/main`, which takes write access to change.

### Nix: binary cache

All local users share one `/nix/store` through nix-daemon. On this host the store already acts as
the cache, so the useful steps are to realize the devshell closures and to protect them from garbage
collection:

- `/nix/var/nix/gcroots/concrete/devshell-default` (116 paths) and `devshell-provers`
  (11.4 GB, Rocq, Isabelle, cvc5), built from `origin/main`'s `flake.lock`;
- `/nix/var/nix/gcroots/concrete/nix-serve-ng` (the server binary).

`concrete-nix-serve.service` serves that store as a **signed** binary cache. It uses nix-serve-ng
from the repository's pinned nixpkgs.

- **Choice of nix-serve-ng.** It serves the live store directly, with no push step, database or
  second copy, and signs narinfos on the fly. Attic adds a server, a database and an upload step,
  and earns that only with several machines that push.
- **Exposure.** It listens on `127.0.0.1:5050` only. The ufw rules are unchanged (OpenSSH only).
  It runs as `DynamicUser`, and the signing key reaches it through systemd `LoadCredential`.
- **Keys.** The secret key is `/etc/nix/concrete-ci-1-cache.sec` (root, 0600). The public key is
  `concrete-ci-1-cache-1:vbcfJrrGDnfqalZ9Tmf0ZsvWY5+UCM77+uHM5aVoyMA=`.
- **Not a substituter on concrete-ci-1 itself.** A local substituter that serves the same store
  can never supply a missing path. It would only add a failed lookup per miss and a new way to fail.
  `/etc/nix/nix.conf` is therefore unchanged, and cache.nixos.org remains the only substituter. A
  second server on the same LAN would add `http://<lan-ip>:5050` (after a ufw rule for that
  interface only) as `extra-substituters`, plus the public key in `extra-trusted-public-keys`.
- **GitHub-hosted runners** never reach it. They keep cache.nixos.org and `actions/cache`. No port
  is opened.
- **The laptop.** The laptop is aarch64-darwin and the server is x86_64-linux, so the server's store
  has nothing a macOS devshell can use. It helps only a Linux builder or VM on the laptop. If one is
  needed, use a tunnel, never a public port:

  ```sh
  ssh -i ~/.ssh/concrete_ci -N -L 5050:127.0.0.1:5050 ubuntu@62.210.216.169 &
  nix build ... --extra-substituters http://127.0.0.1:5050 \
    --extra-trusted-public-keys 'concrete-ci-1-cache-1:vbcfJrrGDnfqalZ9Tmf0ZsvWY5+UCM77+uHM5aVoyMA='
  ```

  `--extra-substituters` is honored only for a trusted user, or when it is listed in the laptop's
  `trusted-substituters`.

### Measured (2026-10-22)

| Build | Time | Modules rebuilt |
|---|---|---|
| Server, cold `lake build` (elan Lean, origin/main) | 105 s | 239/242 |
| Server, seeded, same sources | 1 s copy + 0.2 s | 0 |
| Server, seeded from main, building the previous main commit or wip/ci-tiering | 1 s + 28 s | 9 |
| Laptop, seeded (APFS clone, 0 s) from a stale main checkout, building wip/ci-tiering | 85 s (load ~25) | 178 |
| GitHub-hosted `Build` job, lean-action prior-commit cache (run 39775852735) | 240 s | 148 |
| GitHub-hosted downstream jobs, exact `.lake/build` hit | 20–35 s setup | 0 |

A seed is only as warm as its source is recent. The server seed tracks `origin/main` within 10
minutes, so a self-hosted job pays only for its own diff. A laptop worktree seeded from a stale main
checkout pays for everything that differs.
To publish the record, `report.sh --export docs/project/landing_log/` copies the per-train files
into the tree for a documentation-only commit. Per-train files never conflict on rebase. That
directory is what R-0547's generated median, next to the candidate table, will read.

## Readiness

A branch is **ready** when all of the following hold:

- it is on origin, named `wip/<name>`;
- its head commit is not in main;
- its head carries both notes below, published on origin.

| notes ref | lines | writer |
|---|---|---|
| `refs/notes/concrete-review` | `verdict: clean\|findings\|blocked`, `rounds: N`, `reviewer: codex`, `utc: ...`, `base: <sha>`, `pieces: N` | `scripts/review/review.sh` (`scripts/review/verify.sh` reads it) |
| `refs/notes/concrete-gates` | `gates: pass\|fail`, `selected: N`, `utc: ...` | `scripts/land/ready.sh` |

One `key: value` per line; when a key repeats, the last line wins. `scripts/land/notes.sh` reads and
writes the notes (`get`, `put`, `ready`). `put` builds each write on its own temporary ref on top
of the remote's notes tip, pushes that commit without force, and retries on a race. Concurrent
writers therefore never drop each other's notes, including writers that share one clone or its
worktrees.

**Decision: candidates are `wip/*` branches whose head carries both notes. There is no `ready/*`
prefix.** The reasons:

- **A note is bound to a commit.** A new push starts with no notes, so "ready at the exact head"
  needs no extra rule, and no ready branch can go stale.
- **Agents already push `wip/*`.** The pre-push hook's backup tier keeps those pushes cheap, and
  marking a branch ready is a metadata write, not another branch to keep in step.
- **The ready list is a query**, so nothing has to be cleaned up after a landing.

`wip/land-*` is reserved for the remote leg's temporary refs and is never a candidate.

To mark a branch ready, push it, then run `scripts/land/ready.sh wip/<name>` from a clean
checkout at its head. It runs the review, the impact gates and the remote full set in parallel,
then publishes the gates note: `gates: pass` only when every leg that ran passed. A failed review
leg also publishes `gates: fail`, so an older clean verdict cannot keep the head ready. It exits 0
when the head is ready.

## Pipelining

The review, the impact gates and the remote full set do not depend on each other. `ready.sh` and
`train.sh check` therefore start all three together and wait for all of them. The wall time
becomes the slowest leg instead of the sum. Both scripts record each leg's duration, the wall time
and the sum: `ready.sh` in `<git-common-dir>/concrete-landing/ready.tsv`, and `check` as the
`check-legs` row of the train log.

**Measured on 2026-10-22, on branch `wip/landing-bot`.** Two runs of `ready.sh`, each with its legs
started together:

| run | review (Codex) | impact | remote full set | wall | one after another |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| laptop, `f55935ac` | 117 s | 51 s | lost when the laptop lost its network | n/a | n/a |
| concrete-ci-1, `93aa7ac5` | not run (no Codex login for `dev`) | 69 s | 5239 s, red | 5239 s | 5308 s |

The server was shared with other full runs at a load average of 15 to 32.

- **Saving.** The impact gates (69 s) and the review (117 s, from the laptop run) finish inside the
  remote leg, so about 3 minutes come off every readiness check. Measured: 69 s with the impact
  leg alone, and 186 s with the review included.
- **What dominates.** The remote full set is about 98% of the cost: 87 minutes on a loaded host.
  Pipelining removes the queue in front of it, not the leg itself. The next lever is the full
  set's own time (R-0546 caches, R-0541 dedicated runners) and keeping concurrent full runs off
  the same host.
- **Why the remote leg was red.** Five gates failed:
  - `check_phase6c_observability.sh` #5 and `check_campaign_summary_golden.sh` refuse to run in a
    linked worktree ("Run it in a clone"), which is how that run was made. `run-at-ref.sh` now
    uses `clone --local`.
  - `check_example_outputs.sh` under valgrind, `run_tests.sh` and `check_external_checker.sh`
    were not examined.

  This branch changes only `scripts/land`, one build-free gate with its registration, and this
  document. CI on a train is where those three would be settled.
- **Not counted.** Before pipelining, a human orchestrator usually started the review, the impact
  gates and the remote set by hand in sequence, and each hand-off waited for someone to notice
  the previous result. The scripts remove that wait, and this table does not measure it.

## Configuration
do_negative() {
  if [ -n "${SKIP[negative]:-}" ]; then row negative skipped "--skip"; SKIPPED=1; return 0; fi
  local rid rsha
  rid="$(cd "$C" && sed -n 's/^run_id=//p' "$(git rev-parse --git-common-dir)/concrete-landing/$NAME/train.env" | tail -1)"
  rsha="$(gh run view "$rid" -R "${ACCEPT_REPO:-lambdaclass/concrete}" --json headSha 2>/dev/null | python3 -I -c 'import json,sys; print(json.load(sys.stdin).get("headSha",""))' 2>/dev/null)"
  if [ "$rsha" != "$HEAD" ]; then row neg:run failed "run ${rid:-?} is at ${rsha:-?}, not the train head $HEAD: the controls would test another SHA"; FAILED=1; return 0; fi
  row neg:run passed "run $rid is at the train head ${HEAD:0:10}"
  make_tamper_gh
  local req; req="$(sed 's/#.*//; s/^[[:space:]]*//; s/[[:space:]]*$//' "$C/required-checks.txt" 2>/dev/null | sed '/^$/d' | head -1)"
  if [ -z "$req" ]; then row negative failed "no required-checks.txt in the candidate"; FAILED=1; return 0; fi
  local mode cmd out rc want tlog
  for mode in drop skipped cancelled failure sha; do
    for cmd in watch land; do
      tlog="$WORK/tamper-$mode-$cmd.log"; : > "$tlog"
      case "$mode:$cmd" in
        drop:*) want="missing: $req" ;;
        skipped:*) want="skipped: $req" ;;
        cancelled:*) want="cancelled: $req" ;;
        failure:*) want="failure: $req" ;;
        sha:watch) want="says nothing about this train" ;;
        sha:land) want="not the train HEAD" ;;
      esac
      if [ "$cmd" = watch ]; then
        out="$(cd "$C" && ACCEPT_TAMPER=$mode ACCEPT_REQ="$req" ACCEPT_TAMPER_LOG="$tlog" LAND_GH="$WORK/tamper-gh" bash scripts/land/train.sh watch "$NAME" --interval 1 --timeout 1 2>&1)"; rc=$?
      else
        out="$(cd "$C" && ACCEPT_TAMPER=$mode ACCEPT_REQ="$req" ACCEPT_TAMPER_LOG="$tlog" LAND_GH="$WORK/tamper-gh" bash scripts/land/train.sh --dry-run land "$NAME" 2>&1)"; rc=$?
      fi
      if [ "$rc" = 0 ]; then
        row "neg:$mode" failed "$cmd ACCEPTED a tampered run ($req)"; FAILED=1
      elif [ ! -s "$tlog" ]; then
        row "neg:$mode" failed "$cmd refused, but the wrapper changed nothing: no evidence of the mutation"; FAILED=1
      elif ! printf '%s\n' "$out" | awk -v w="$want" 'index($0, w) {f=1} END {exit !f}'; then
        row "neg:$mode" failed "$cmd refused for another reason (expected '$want'): $(printf '%s\n' "$out" | tail -n 1 | cut -c1-80)"; FAILED=1
      else
        row "neg:$mode" passed "$cmd refused: $want"
      fi
    done
  done
}

# A2i: unifying the Concrete Lean spec into one Core (plan, draft 3)

Status: proposal, accepted by Codex and Kimi in review round 3 (agy accepted round 2; its round-3 rerun on this text is pending); the spec worker carries it out as stage A2i (R-0571). The Lean spec stays the only home of the rules: this file is the work plan and is retired when A2i is done. Draft 3 folds in
the Codex, agy and Kimi delta reviews of draft 2 (section 7), the agreed slice order
(the [vertical slices](../../ROADMAP.md#vertical-slices), all four reviewers, 2026-11-06) and the owner decisions of 2026-11-06 relayed by
the coordinator (section 4), and the coordinator's answers to draft 3's questions S1–S3 (section
5.1). Drafts 1 and 2, the archive copies of the inputs and the raw review outputs are not kept in
the repository; the exact revisions below are the inputs. I checked each kept reviewer claim against the files. **Revised 2026-11-08** for the owner's foundation decisions: the summary's build order, S1 (5.1), the module graph (2.2) and section 6 from step 3 on now write the foundation C1–C10 of [FOUNDATION.md](FOUNDATION.md) first and the slices on top of it.

---

## 0. Summary

The spec header calls its parts "small Core fragment[s]" (`Spec.lean:41-42`). Each fragment
re-invents the same things:

- 8 keyed-lookup functions plus 4 update and 2 remove functions;
- 6 statement representations;
- 7 result or outcome types;
- 5 evaluators with 3 fuel conventions;
- 6 "Ok" judgments;
- 2 operation tables;
- 2 loan models;
- 2 static-fact models;
- 2 encodings of allocator dependency.

There are also four instrumentation domains with one erasure proof between them.

Nothing joins these fragments:

- `Cleanup`, `Borrow`, `Brand` and `Authority` import nothing.
- `Semantics` imports the static `Ownership` (`Spec/Semantics.lean:1`).
- Three normative modules import the examples module: `Rep`, `Observation` and `Slice0`.

**The plan.** It replaces the fragments with one Core:

- one syntax, with a `SiteId` on every operation;
- typed identities;
- one `Ty` and one `Val`;
- one primitive-contract table (Q9.7);
- one clock-indexed semantics over explicit instrumented state (Q9.9);
- one outcome and event model;
- one evidence domain;
- one context with derived locks;
- one admission judgment with named premises;
- one checker returning `CheckResult`;
- one grammar as data;
- one facts vocabulary;
- one semantic contract.

The four design theorems keep their names and their full conclusions.

**Build order.** Foundations first, with no change of meaning (phase A); then the foundation's
semantics, contracts C1–C10, into which every old feature fragment is ported whole and deleted in
that commit (phase B); then closure and the owner's freeze of the foundation (phase D). After the
freeze the Core grows slice by slice on top of it, in the agreed order of the
[vertical slices](../../ROADMAP.md#vertical-slices)
([row](../decisions.md#decided-project-vertical-slices-2026-11-06)): slice 0, slice 1 (the Q8.2
linear empty struct, with explicit `abort()` and the refused `200 + 56` control), slice 2 (all
integers), slice 3 (bool, `if`, `let mut`, the octagon checker), slice 4 (loops and termination),
slice 5 (functions), slice 6 (authority), and onward (phase C). The library builds and passes the
proof checks after every commit.

**The foundation first, then per slice** (owner, 2026-11-07: "ok to option b then for freeze the
foundation>?"; [decision](../decisions.md#decided-2026-11-07-foundation-first)). A2i writes the
shared foundation, the contracts C1–C10 of [FOUNDATION.md](FOUNDATION.md) (approved 2026-11-08:
"ok all recommended"), in Lean and ports every old feature fragment into it; the owner freezes that
foundation at A2c, before slice 1 builds on it. Each slice's spec side is then written on top of
the frozen foundation, in slice order, without rewriting it. This answers S1 (5.1), which had
proposed writing every slice's spec side before A2c. The order in which the contracts are written
is section 6. The old fragments are ported in the foundation steps (section 6, phase B), not at their slices.

The owner questions of draft 2 (layout, rule-ID spelling, spec version) and the slice plan's owner
questions are decided (sections 4 and 5.1).

## 1. Inventory: concepts defined more than once

### 1.1 Keyed lookup, update and removal

| Copy | Where |
|---|---|
| `lookupF` (generic) | `Spec/Syntax.lean:23` |
| `envFind` | `Spec/Semantics.lean:62` |
| `ctxFind` (by `Entry.name`) | `Spec/Ownership.lean:35` |
| `Borrow.find` (`Nat` keys, `Place` values) | `Spec/Borrow.lean:42` |
| `Borrow.getN` (`Nat` keys, any value) — the same pattern, polymorphic, in the same file | `Spec/Borrow.lean:404` |
| `Brand.findL` | `Spec/Brand.lean:32` |
| `findDecl`, `Prog.find` (via `List.find?`) | `Spec/Syntax.lean:87`, `Spec/Authority.lean:151` |
| updates: `envMove`, `ctxMark`, `putN`, `mapPlaces` | `Spec/Semantics.lean:67`, `Spec/Ownership.lean:40`, `Spec/Borrow.lean:409,46` |
| removals: `Borrow.remove`, `Brand.removeL` | `Spec/Borrow.lean:48`, `Spec/Brand.lean:36` |
| lemmas proved per copy | `Proofs/Basics.lean:11,20` (`ctxFind_mem`, `lookupF_map`); `Proofs/Borrow.lean:11,21,40,450` (`find_mem` and the `getN`/`putN` lemmas); `Proofs/Brand.lean:11,20,31,372,378`; also `tys_lookup` and `ctxMark_tys` (`Proofs/Slice1.lean`) |

**Unified:** one `AList` API (`lookup`, `update`, `erase`, `map`) in `Spec/Basics.lean`, with its lemmas
once in `Proofs/Basics.lean`. Every map is keyed by a typed identity (1.2).

### 1.2 Identities and sites

| Copy | Where |
|---|---|
| `Var` (hint + number): the only typed identity | `Spec/Syntax.lean:15` |
| struct and field names as `String` | `Spec/Syntax.lean:11,78-81` |
| places and roots as `Nat` | `Spec/Borrow.lean:33,40` |
| allocator variables, heap values and brands as `Nat` | `Spec/Brand.lean:44,56-74` |
| resources, loans and loop labels as `Nat` | `Spec/Cleanup.lean:31,34,60` |
| functions as `String`, with `"main"` looked up by string | `Spec/Authority.lean:140,151,206` |
| `"main"` refused as a struct name, by string | `Spec/Ownership.lean:201` |
| grants as `Nat` positions and launcher numbers | `Spec/Authority.lean:131,221,237` |
| operation sites: none in Core | README O1/O4; slice 0 mints `SiteId` beside Core (`slice0-README.md:80`) |

The facts decision requires typed identities: module, function and instance, with no bare-name
collisions (`dec:729`). Rule 13 requires that `#[proof]` resolve to a typed theorem identity
(`dec:640`).

**Unified:** `Spec/Ident.lean` with these identities:

- `Var`, `DeclId`, `FieldId`, `FnId`, `ModuleId`, `InstanceId`;
- `LoopId` (labels, Q9.3);
- `BrandId` and `LineageId` (the memory decision's allocator lineage);
- `LoanId`, `GrantId`, `BlockId` (Q3.7);
- `CapVarId` (capability parameters, Q5.1–Q5.4; used from slice 11);
- `TheoremId`;
- `SiteId`, one per operation and per refusable statement.

Every operation node carries a `SiteId`; elaboration (O1) maps it to a span. Diagnostics, facts,
traps, leakage records and discharge tokens all key on it.

### 1.3 Types and linearity

| Copy | Where | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `IntTy`, `STy`, `Ty` | `Spec/Syntax.lean:31,64,70` | struct fields are scalars only |
| `Brand.STy` (`alloc β`, `heap β`) | `Spec/Brand.lean:44` | the same name as `ConcreteSpec.STy`, with a different meaning |
| `Authority.GrantTy` | `Spec/Authority.lean:96` | |
| `Borrow.Place` flags `mutable`, `owner` | `Spec/Borrow.lean:32-36` | stand in for a type |
| `Borrow.Source` | `Spec/Borrow.lean:133-136` | run-time data (array length, text bytes), which `RangeFacts` then also uses as a static fact (`:177`) |
| linearity | `Ty.isCopy` `Spec/Syntax.lean:91`; `Val.isCopy` `Spec/Semantics.lean:72` (the same declaration lookup again); prose `Spec/Brand.lean:42` | three encodings. `Place.owner` (`Spec/Borrow.lean:35`) is root-versus-view ownership metadata, not linearity; it becomes the entry's `root` field (1.12) |

**Unified:** one `Ty` in `Spec/Types.lean` with these constructors:

- `scalar`;
- `struct` and `enum`, the flat nominal data of Q9.2 and Q9.4, whose fields are any `Ty`;
- `array T n` and `text`;
- `alloc β` and `heap β T`, with brands as a separate erased kind (Q1.1);
- `view m T`, second-class (Q3.1–Q3.3);
- `grant g`;
- `Result` and `Option` as ordinary enum declarations;
- `secret T` on scalars (R-0596), from slice 7, within A2i.

`Ty` is complete for every type the old fragments already use (alloc, heap, views, grants, text)
from phase A, ahead of the slices that expose them in source: `Fragment.surface` gates the source,
not the Core.

There is one `Ty.isCopy`. One recursive `Ty.brands`, over struct and enum fields, gives the
allocator dependencies of Q1.5 and Q1.6. `Val.isCopy` goes away: the run-time environment keeps
each binding's declared type as instrumentation.

### 1.4 Values and stores

| Copy | Where |
|---|---|
| `Scalar`, `Val` (struct of scalars) | `Spec/Syntax.lean:98`, `Spec/Semantics.lean:15` |
| `Brand.Val`, `Brand.EVal` | `Spec/Brand.lean:103,196` |
| `Borrow.Mem` (untyped `List Nat` arrays), `VInfo`, `Views` | `Spec/Borrow.lean:414,420,427` |
| text as `List Nat` | `Spec/Borrow.lean:136` |
| `Rep.MByte` (not used by any evaluator) | `Spec/Rep.lean:28` |
| grant handles as `List Nat` | `Spec/Authority.lean:237` |

Value typing is restated per fragment: `SVTy` (`Spec/Semantics.lean:201`) and `RepVal`
(`Spec/Rep.lean`) say a value fits a type. `ViewsOk` (`Proofs/Borrow.lean:467`, views against
memory) and `Brand.Rel` (`Proofs/Brand.lean:198`, a context and environment against a brand
realization) are state invariants built on value typing.

**Unified:** one recursive `Val` in `Spec/Values.lean` and one `Val.HasTy`. `SVTy` and `RepVal`
become it; `ViewsOk` and `Rel` become invariants of the one semantic state, stated with `Val.HasTy`
(they are not replaced by value typing alone). Atom typing and evaluation stay subcases of
expression typing and evaluation, each defined once. One store of blocks with provenance
(Q3.7) in the semantic state. `Layout` relates values to bytes (Q3.11). Brands are ghost data on
handles (1.9).

### 1.5 Statement languages and programs

There are six statement representations:

| Statements | Where |
|---|---|
| slice-1 `Atom`, `Expr`, `Binder`, `Stmt`, `Program` | `Spec/Syntax.lean:112-147` |
| slice-0 `Atom`, `Expr`, `Stmt`, `Program`, with `toCore` and `InSlice0 := ∃ q, toCore q = p` | `Spec/Slice0.lean:34-75` |
| `Borrow.Stmt` | `Spec/Borrow.lean:69` |
| `Borrow.VStmt`, a second language in the same file | `Spec/Borrow.lean:433` |
| `Brand.Stmt` | `Spec/Brand.lean:56` |
| `Authority.Stmt`, `Fn`, `Prog` | `Spec/Authority.lean:129,139,148` |

`Cleanup` has no statements at all: it models `Deferred` records and `Exit` values
(`Spec/Cleanup.lean:48,80`).

**Unified:** one ANF Core syntax (Q9.1, Q9.3–Q9.5, Q9.8) in `Spec/Syntax.lean`. It has:

- atoms, and operation nodes through the primitive table, each carrying a `SiteId`;
- `let`, `let _` and destructuring;
- `match` on flat data;
- place writes;
- `borrow [mut] p [from a until b] as v { … } [else { … }]`, and the distinct heap-open case (Q9.5);
- `defer` calls, whose by-value arguments are read when the call runs (`dec:1250`);
- nested scopes;
- `while L { C } do { B }` (Q9.3), with the attributes as data: `#[invariant]`, `#[variant]`,
  `#[may_not_terminate("…")]`;
- `if`;
- `break L`, `continue L` and `return`;
- calls with explicit capability arguments (Q5.1);
- brand binders, explicit `pack` and lexical `unpack` (Q1.2, Q1.3);
- `abort`.

`Fn` carries its parameters, grant parameters, `with(...)`, `trusted`, `#[variant]` and
`#[proof(TheoremId)]`. A `Program` is its declarations plus its functions. `main` declares its grants
as parameters (STMT-7, `dec:1227`). `Spec/Syntax.lean:140` ("may declare no grants") should be
reworded: it allows a grant-free slice, but it reads like a prohibition.

**The slice-0 syntax.** It stops being a second Core syntax. A slice is two decidable predicates:

- `Fragment.surface`: the grammar productions the slice supports;
- `Fragment.core`: the Core constructors the slice supports.

Both live in `Spec/Fragments.lean`. The decided "not supported in this slice" refusal is a
surface-level classification: a decided form whose production lies outside `Fragment.surface`
(`dec:829-835`). An invalid program is refused by the grammar or by admission. The Core predicate
scopes the slice theorems.

The `wip/slice0` design kept `toCore`, retargeted at the integrated Core
(`slice0-README.md:615`). Decided internally (I3, 2026-11-06): one Core syntax; slice 0 uses the
fragment predicates, and `Spec/Slice0.lean`'s syntax and `toCore` are deleted.

### 1.6 Outcomes, results, traps and failure

| Copy | Where | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `R` (ok, trap, stuck) | `Spec/Semantics.lean:46` | |
| `Outcome` (exit, trap, stuck, outOfFuel) | `Spec/Semantics.lean:54` | |
| `Trap` (`arithmeticOverflow` only), `Stuck` | `Spec/Semantics.lean:32,38` | no site |
| `Brand.GR`, `Brand.ER` | `Spec/Brand.lean:147,210` | |
| `Borrow.VR` | `Spec/Borrow.lean:447` | `trap` has no kind |
| `Borrow.RangeStep.trap` | `Spec/Borrow.lean:158` (the constructor at `:164`) | |
| `Cleanup.Result`, plus the oracle `ok : Deferred → Bool` | `Spec/Cleanup.lean:111,119` | trap kind lost |
| `Authority.Trace.complete : Bool` | `Spec/Authority.lean:228` | conflates out-of-fuel with stuck |
| `checkedAdd` | `Spec/Semantics.lean:115` | |
| `Observation.trapped k` | `Spec/Observation.lean:43` | no site (O4) |

**Unified** in `Spec/Semantics.lean`, following Q6.7, `dec:1224` and the no-hidden-traps decision:

```
inductive TrapKind | arith (e : ArithmeticError) | index | range | byteAt | abort | …   -- placeholder names: the owner's (question 9)
def TrapKind.implicit : TrapKind → Bool          -- every kind except `abort`
inductive Outcome
  | exit (code : Nat) (h : code < 256)   -- or Fin 256; keep what wip/slice0 consumes
  | trap (k : TrapKind) (s : SiteId)     -- status 134 for every kind (Q7.10); abort is a trap (dec:1224)
  | exhausted (r : TargetResource)       -- target-only (stack, Q14): a named premise, never an allocation failure
  | stuck (w : Stuck)                    -- unsafe stuckness, ruled out by admission
  | outOfFuel                            -- the clock ran out; inconclusive, not divergence
```

`ArithmeticError { Overflow, DivisionByZero, InvalidShift }` is defined once, in `Spec/Types.lean`.
It is the error payload of the `checked_*` operations. Whether a trap kind wraps it, and the trap
kinds' names and renderings, wait for the owner (foundation question 9).
Allocation failure is a value, `AllocFailure<T>` (Q1.8, Q1.9), not an outcome.

One result type replaces `R`, `GR`, `ER`, `VR` and `Cleanup.Result`. Primitives return a
primitive-local result (1.11), which the semantics maps to sited outcomes.

### 1.7 Events, traces and observations

| Copy | Where |
|---|---|
| `Cleanup.Event` (a call, or the release of a loan) | `Spec/Cleanup.lean:105` |
| `Authority.Event` (an operation on a grant), `Trace` | `Spec/Authority.lean:219,228` |
| `vrun`'s ad-hoc output list `out` | `Spec/Borrow.lean:457` |
| `Observation` | `Spec/Observation.lean:37` |

**Unified:** two channels, kept apart.

- **Public events.** One `Event` type in Core with an explicit observable-event kind (decided
  2026-11-06): `start`, `exit`, and the operations on grants (with Q7.9's request/response split when
  blocking arrives). Only these, with the outcome, feed `Observation`. The Start/Exit trace of
  slice 6 is these kinds.
- **The ghost log** (1.9). It records cleanup calls, loan ends, alloc and destroy (each with lineage,
  size and call frame), release sites and leakage records. It is instrumentation, never observed.

The event kind is extensible (SPARK row). A secret wipe is a **public** event kind (R-0602), added
at slice 7: erasure keeps it and every later preservation theorem must preserve it, which is what
protects a wipe from being optimized away. It is not in the ghost log.

Making alloc, free or cleanup a public observation would change what programs observably do, so
the plan does not do it.
